I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I need way less sleep and feel wired or unstoppable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly. Yeah. I mean, it really started after I got fired… that was about thirteen months ago now, I think. It’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just normal stress, anyone would be spun up after that.

But down, like sad all day? I don’t know. It kind of comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, it really started after I got fired… that was about thirteen months ago now, I think. It’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just normal stress, anyone would be spun up after that.\n\nBut then even when I found contract work, it didn’t really settle down. I kept thinking, well, once the next thing starts I’ll calm down, and then once I got something I was still… I don’t know, still carrying it around. And then you start comparing yourself to other people from old teams, LinkedIn updates and all that, which is probably not helpful—sorry. But yeah, the firing was the point where it started, and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the reason I came in, honestly. Once it starts, I don’t seem to be able to shut it off.\n\nIt can start with something small, like a client not responding to an email, and then within a few minutes I’m thinking, okay, they’re unhappy, I’m going to lose the contract, then I won’t keep up with the divorce payments, and then I’ll never get back into a real leadership role again. And then I’m checking my phone or replaying what I wrote, which is stupid because staring at it doesn’t make them answer faster.\n\nSo, yes. It’s hard to control. It just kind of runs away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still… I mean, I still like things when I actually do them, I think. It’s more that I don’t always get around to them.\n\nLike I used to watch games or mess around with some side tech projects, and then I’ll open my laptop and somehow end up reading some article about the job market or comparing salaries, which is not fun, obviously. But that’s not the same as not enjoying anything, right?\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of pleasure. It’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. That’s a heavy word, you know?\n\nI have moments where I think, “What if this just doesn’t turn around?” and then I’ll start thinking about work stuff, or people I used to manage who are now doing great, and I get kind of stuck in that comparison loop. But I don’t know if that’s hopeless exactly, or just… feeling worn down.\n\nI guess I haven’t really put that label on it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’ll start with something small, like a client not responding to an email, and then within minutes I’m already thinking, well, that means we’re losing the contract, and then I’m behind on divorce payments, and then I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again.\n\nIt’s like my mind builds this whole chain before I can stop it. And I know some of it sounds like a jump, but in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying like this about many different parts of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just work. I mean, work is the easiest place for it to start, because that’s where the emails and contracts and recruiters are, but then it jumps.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about some contract thing, and suddenly I’m on mortgage payments, or whether people around Bethesda in the tech circles see me as… I don’t know, damaged goods or something. And then if a recruiter takes too long to answer an email, it doesn’t feel like just a delayed email. It feels like proof that the whole career door is closing. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it lands in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized or wired and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like energized in a good way. I mean, I have nights where I’m up late, but it’s more like I’m stuck thinking, not like I’m suddenly full of energy and fine with no sleep.\n\nThere was this stretch where I kept checking messages and kind of pacing around the kitchen, but that was because I was waiting on something work-related and then I started thinking about bills, and then—sorry, I’m drifting. I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I need way less sleep and feel wired or unstoppable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, yeah, especially when I start thinking about where I thought I’d be by now versus where things are. But “most days” is hard to answer.\n\nSome days I’m just… preoccupied, I guess. Like I’ll sit down to do one thing and then suddenly I’m thinking about three other things, and then I’m irritated at myself for not being able to just handle it like I used to. But down, like sad all day? I don’t know. It kind of comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, it really started after I got fired… that was about thirteen months ago now, I think. It’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just normal stress, anyone would be spun up after that.\n\nBut then even when I found contract work, it didn’t really settle down. I kept thinking, well, once the next thing starts I’ll calm down, and then once I got something I was still… I don’t know, still carrying it around. But yeah, the firing was the point where it started, and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the reason I came in, honestly. And then I’m checking my phone or replaying what I wrote, which is stupid because staring at it doesn’t make them answer faster.\n\nSo, yes. It’s hard to control. It just kind of runs away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still… I mean, I still like things when I actually do them, I think. It’s more that I don’t always get around to them.\n\nLike I used to watch games or mess around with some side tech projects, and then I’ll open my laptop and somehow end up reading some article about the job market or comparing salaries, which is not fun, obviously. But that’s not the same as not enjoying anything, right?\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of pleasure. It’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. That’s a heavy word, you know?\n\nI have moments where I think, “What if this just doesn’t turn around?” and then I’ll start thinking about work stuff, or people I used to manage who are now doing great, and I get kind of stuck in that comparison loop. But I don’t know if that’s hopeless exactly, or just… feeling worn down.\n\nI guess I haven’t really put that label on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, in the moment it feels totally justified, like I’m just being realistic. But then if I say it out loud… yeah, it’s bigger than the situation.\n\nLike one delayed email from a recruiter, and my mind is already off to, okay, they’re not interested, no one’s interested, my contract work is going to dry up, then how am I going to handle the mortgage, and then I start thinking about people in the Bethesda tech circles and whether they see me as damaged goods now. Which sounds dramatic when I’m saying it here.\n\nIt’s not like the email itself deserves all that. It’s just where my head goes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… physically, yes. I feel kind of wound up almost all day, honestly.

Once it starts, I don’t seem to be able to shut it off.\n\nIt can start with something small, like a client not responding to an email, and then within a few minutes I’m thinking, okay, they’re unhappy, I’m going to lose the contract, then I won’t keep up with the divorce payments, and then I’ll never get back into a real leadership role again. And then I’m checking my phone or replaying what I wrote, which is stupid because staring at it doesn’t make them answer faster.\n\nSo, yes. It’s hard to control. It just kind of runs away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still… I mean, I still like things when I actually do them, I think. It’s more that I don’t always get around to them.\n\nLike I used to watch games or mess around with some side tech projects, and then I’ll open my laptop and somehow end up reading some article about the job market or comparing salaries, which is not fun, obviously. But that’s not the same as not enjoying anything, right?\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of pleasure. It’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. That’s a heavy word, you know?\n\nI have moments where I think, “What if this just doesn’t turn around?” and then I’ll start thinking about work stuff, or people I used to manage who are now doing great, and I get kind of stuck in that comparison loop. But I don’t know if that’s hopeless exactly, or just… feeling worn down.\n\nI guess I haven’t really put that label on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, in the moment it feels totally justified, like I’m just being realistic. But then if I say it out loud… yeah, it’s bigger than the situation.\n\nLike one delayed email from a recruiter, and my mind is already off to, okay, they’re not interested, no one’s interested, my contract work is going to dry up, then how am I going to handle the mortgage, and then I start thinking about people in the Bethesda tech circles and whether they see me as damaged goods now. Which sounds dramatic when I’m saying it here.\n\nIt’s not like the email itself deserves all that. It’s just where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling physically restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… physically, yes. I feel kind of wound up almost all day, honestly.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are just locked up, and I’m sort of bracing for something — like bad news is about to come in, even if nothing’s actually happening. I’ll be on a call and realize I’ve been bouncing my leg the whole time, or I’ll get up and pace around the house for no real reason.\n\nI mean, sometimes I tell myself I’m just “thinking better” while moving around, but… yeah, it’s more like I’m on edge.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. The circumstances changed a little, but the worrying just kind of… stayed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I can’t really shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with something small, like a client not responding to an email, and then within minutes I’m already thinking, well, that means we’re losing the contract, and then I’m behind on divorce payments, and then I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again.\n\nIt’s like my mind builds this whole chain before I can stop it. And I know some of it sounds like a jump, but in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying like this about many different parts of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just work. I mean, work is the easiest place for it to start, because that’s where the emails and contracts and recruiters are, but then it jumps.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about some contract thing, and suddenly I’m on mortgage payments, or whether people around Bethesda in the tech circles see me as… I don’t know, damaged goods or something. And then if a recruiter takes too long to answer an email, it doesn’t feel like just a delayed email. It feels like proof that the whole career door is closing. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it lands in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized or wired and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like energized in a good way. I mean, I have nights where I’m up late, but it’s more like I’m stuck thinking, not like I’m suddenly full of energy and fine with no sleep.\n\nThere was this stretch where I kept checking messages and kind of pacing around the kitchen, but that was because I was waiting on something work-related and then I started thinking about bills, and then—sorry, I’m drifting. I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I need way less sleep and feel wired or unstoppable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, yeah, especially when I start thinking about where I thought I’d be by now versus where things are. But “most days” is hard to answer.\n\nSome days I’m just… preoccupied, I guess. Like I’ll sit down to do one thing and then suddenly I’m thinking about three other things, and then I’m irritated at myself for not being able to just handle it like I used to. But down, like sad all day? I don’t know. It kind of comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, it really started after I got fired… that was about thirteen months ago now, I think. It’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just normal stress, anyone would be spun up after that.\n\nBut then even when I found contract work, it didn’t really settle down. I kept thinking, well, once the next thing starts I’ll calm down, and then once I got something I was still… I don’t know, still carrying it around. Once it starts, I don’t seem to be able to shut it off.\n\nIt can start with something small, like a client not responding to an email, and then within a few minutes I’m thinking, okay, they’re unhappy, I’m going to lose the contract, then I won’t keep up with the divorce payments, and then I’ll never get back into a real leadership role again. And then I’m checking my phone or replaying what I wrote, which is stupid because staring at it doesn’t make them answer faster.\n\nSo, yes. It’s hard to control. It just kind of runs away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. The circumstances changed a little, but the worrying just kind of… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or easily tired because of all this worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired, yes, but I’m not sure I can say it’s specifically because of the worrying.\n\nI mean, I do get drained, but then again I’m 47 and staring at screens half the day, and even normal work stuff can wipe you out. Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I get into bed and my brain just starts running through things, like replaying how I answered an interview question or how I should’ve said something differently.

And then I’ll check the clock and it’s like 2 or 3 in the morning, which of course makes it worse because then I’m doing the math on how much sleep I can still get. It reminds me of when I used to be on call for system outages—like even if nothing happens, you’re never really fully off. So even on nights where technically I got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t actually rest.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I can’t really shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with something small, like a client not responding to an email, and then within minutes I’m already thinking, well, that means we’re losing the contract, and then I’m behind on divorce payments, and then I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again.\n\nIt’s like my mind builds this whole chain before I can stop it. And I know some of it sounds like a jump, but in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying like this about many different parts of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just work. I mean, work is the easiest place for it to start, because that’s where the emails and contracts and recruiters are, but then it jumps.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about some contract thing, and suddenly I’m on mortgage payments, or whether people around Bethesda in the tech circles see me as… I don’t know, damaged goods or something. And then if a recruiter takes too long to answer an email, it doesn’t feel like just a delayed email. It feels like proof that the whole career door is closing. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it lands in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized or wired and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like energized in a good way. I mean, I have nights where I’m up late, but it’s more like I’m stuck thinking, not like I’m suddenly full of energy and fine with no sleep.\n\nThere was this stretch where I kept checking messages and kind of pacing around the kitchen, but that was because I was waiting on something work-related and then I started thinking about bills, and then—sorry, I’m drifting. I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I need way less sleep and feel wired or unstoppable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, yeah, especially when I start thinking about where I thought I’d be by now versus where things are. But “most days” is hard to answer.\n\nSome days I’m just… preoccupied, I guess. Like I’ll sit down to do one thing and then suddenly I’m thinking about three other things, and then I’m irritated at myself for not being able to just handle it like I used to. But down, like sad all day? I don’t know. It kind of comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, it really started after I got fired… that was about thirteen months ago now, I think. It’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just normal stress, anyone would be spun up after that.\n\nBut then even when I found contract work, it didn’t really settle down. I kept thinking, well, once the next thing starts I’ll calm down, and then once I got something I was still… I don’t know, still carrying it around. And then you start comparing yourself to other people from old teams, LinkedIn updates and all that, which is probably not helpful—sorry. But yeah, the firing was the point where it started, and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the reason I came in, honestly. Once it starts, I don’t seem to be able to shut it off.\n\nIt can start with something small, like a client not responding to an email, and then within a few minutes I’m thinking, okay, they’re unhappy, I’m going to lose the contract, then I won’t keep up with the divorce payments, and then I’ll never get back into a real leadership role again. And then I’m checking my phone or replaying what I wrote, which is stupid because staring at it doesn’t make them answer faster.\n\nSo, yes. It’s hard to control. It just kind of runs away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. But I don’t know if that’s hopeless exactly, or just… feeling worn down.\n\nI guess I haven’t really put that label on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, in the moment it feels totally justified, like I’m just being realistic. But then if I say it out loud… yeah, it’s bigger than the situation.\n\nLike one delayed email from a recruiter, and my mind is already off to, okay, they’re not interested, no one’s interested, my contract work is going to dry up, then how am I going to handle the mortgage, and then I start thinking about people in the Bethesda tech circles and whether they see me as damaged goods now. Which sounds dramatic when I’m saying it here.\n\nIt’s not like the email itself deserves all that. The circumstances changed a little, but the worrying just kind of… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or easily tired because of all this worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired, yes, but I’m not sure I can say it’s specifically because of the worrying.\n\nI mean, I do get drained, but then again I’m 47 and staring at screens half the day, and even normal work stuff can wipe you out. There are days where I’ll sit down after lunch and feel like my battery just dropped to 10%, but I don’t always connect it directly to the worry. It’s all kind of tangled together, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I get into bed and my brain just starts running through things, like replaying how I answered an interview question or how I should’ve said something differently.\n\nAnd then I’ll check the clock and it’s like 2 or 3 in the morning, which of course makes it worse because then I’m doing the math on how much sleep I can still get. It reminds me of when I used to be on call for system outages—like even if nothing happens, you’re never really fully off. So even on nights where technically I got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t actually rest.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… it has. I mean, I try to tell myself I’m still functioning because I’m doing some work, but I’ve missed networking follow-ups that I really shouldn’t have missed. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I can’t really shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with something small, like a client not responding to an email, and then within minutes I’m already thinking, well, that means we’re losing the contract, and then I’m behind on divorce payments, and then I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again.\n\nIt’s like my mind builds this whole chain before I can stop it. And I know some of it sounds like a jump, but in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying like this about many different parts of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just work. I mean, work is the easiest place for it to start, because that’s where the emails and contracts and recruiters are, but then it jumps.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about some contract thing, and suddenly I’m on mortgage payments, or whether people around Bethesda in the tech circles see me as… I don’t know, damaged goods or something. And then if a recruiter takes too long to answer an email, it doesn’t feel like just a delayed email. It feels like proof that the whole career door is closing. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it lands in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized or wired and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like energized in a good way. I mean, I have nights where I’m up late, but it’s more like I’m stuck thinking, not like I’m suddenly full of energy and fine with no sleep.\n\nThere was this stretch where I kept checking messages and kind of pacing around the kitchen, but that was because I was waiting on something work-related and then I started thinking about bills, and then—sorry, I’m drifting. I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I need way less sleep and feel wired or unstoppable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, yeah, especially when I start thinking about where I thought I’d be by now versus where things are. But “most days” is hard to answer.\n\nSome days I’m just… preoccupied, I guess. Like I’ll sit down to do one thing and then suddenly I’m thinking about three other things, and then I’m irritated at myself for not being able to just handle it like I used to. But down, like sad all day? I don’t know. It kind of comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, it really started after I got fired… that was about thirteen months ago now, I think. It’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just normal stress, anyone would be spun up after that.\n\nBut then even when I found contract work, it didn’t really settle down. I kept thinking, well, once the next thing starts I’ll calm down, and then once I got something I was still… I don’t know, still carrying it around. And then you start comparing yourself to other people from old teams, LinkedIn updates and all that, which is probably not helpful—sorry. But yeah, the firing was the point where it started, and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the reason I came in, honestly. Once it starts, I don’t seem to be able to shut it off.\n\nIt can start with something small, like a client not responding to an email, and then within a few minutes I’m thinking, okay, they’re unhappy, I’m going to lose the contract, then I won’t keep up with the divorce payments, and then I’ll never get back into a real leadership role again. And then I’m checking my phone or replaying what I wrote, which is stupid because staring at it doesn’t make them answer faster.\n\nSo, yes. It’s hard to control. It just kind of runs away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still… I mean, I still like things when I actually do them, I think. It’s more that I don’t always get around to them.\n\nLike I used to watch games or mess around with some side tech projects, and then I’ll open my laptop and somehow end up reading some article about the job market or comparing salaries, which is not fun, obviously. It’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. But I don’t know if that’s hopeless exactly, or just… feeling worn down.\n\nI guess I haven’t really put that label on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, in the moment it feels totally justified, like I’m just being realistic. But then if I say it out loud… yeah, it’s bigger than the situation.\n\nLike one delayed email from a recruiter, and my mind is already off to, okay, they’re not interested, no one’s interested, my contract work is going to dry up, then how am I going to handle the mortgage, and then I start thinking about people in the Bethesda tech circles and whether they see me as damaged goods now. Which sounds dramatic when I’m saying it here.\n\nIt’s not like the email itself deserves all that. It’s just where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling physically restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… physically, yes. I feel kind of wound up almost all day, honestly.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are just locked up, and I’m sort of bracing for something — like bad news is about to come in, even if nothing’s actually happening. But I don’t know if that’s hopeless exactly, or just… feeling worn down.\n\nI guess I haven’t really put that label on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, in the moment it feels totally justified, like I’m just being realistic. But then if I say it out loud… yeah, it’s bigger than the situation.\n\nLike one delayed email from a recruiter, and my mind is already off to, okay, they’re not interested, no one’s interested, my contract work is going to dry up, then how am I going to handle the mortgage, and then I start thinking about people in the Bethesda tech circles and whether they see me as damaged goods now. Which sounds dramatic when I’m saying it here.\n\nIt’s not like the email itself deserves all that. It’s just where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling physically restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… physically, yes. I feel kind of wound up almost all day, honestly.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are just locked up, and I’m sort of bracing for something — like bad news is about to come in, even if nothing’s actually happening. I’ll be on a call and realize I’ve been bouncing my leg the whole time, or I’ll get up and pace around the house for no real reason.\n\nI mean, sometimes I tell myself I’m just “thinking better” while moving around, but… yeah, it’s more like I’m on edge.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. It really started when I got fired, which was about thirteen months ago now.\n\nAnd I thought once I picked up the contract work it would settle down, because, you know, at least I wasn’t just sitting there refreshing job boards and LinkedIn all day — though I still did that more than I should’ve — but it didn’t really let up. The circumstances changed a little, but the worrying just kind of… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or easily tired because of all this worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired, yes, but I’m not sure I can say it’s specifically because of the worrying.\n\nI mean, I do get drained, but then again I’m 47 and staring at screens half the day, and even normal work stuff can wipe you out. There are days where I’ll sit down after lunch and feel like my battery just dropped to 10%, but I don’t always connect it directly to the worry. It’s all kind of tangled together, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I get into bed and my brain just starts running through things, like replaying how I answered an interview question or how I should’ve said something differently.\n\nAnd then I’ll check the clock and it’s like 2 or 3 in the morning, which of course makes it worse because then I’m doing the math on how much sleep I can still get. It reminds me of when I used to be on call for system outages—like even if nothing happens, you’re never really fully off. So even on nights where technically I got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t actually rest.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I try to tell myself I’m still functioning because I’m doing some work, but I’ve missed networking follow-ups that I really shouldn’t have missed. Like I’ll write the email in my head ten different ways and then not send it because I’m thinking of all the ways it could come off wrong.\n\nAnd with client stuff, I’ve delayed deliverables because I get stuck anticipating everything that might go sideways instead of just finishing the thing. It’s stupid because in my line of work you’re supposed to plan for failure points, but this is different — it’s like I can’t stop planning for every possible disaster.\n\nOutside of work too… I’ve pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. I used to go more regularly, and now I just feel tense and irritable, and honestly kind of embarrassed to be around people like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it lands in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized or wired and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like energized in a good way. I mean, I have nights where I’m up late, but it’s more like I’m stuck thinking, not like I’m suddenly full of energy and fine with no sleep.\n\nThere was this stretch where I kept checking messages and kind of pacing around the kitchen, but that was because I was waiting on something work-related and then I started thinking about bills, and then—sorry, I’m drifting. I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I need way less sleep and feel wired or unstoppable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I get discouraged, yeah, especially when I start thinking about where I thought I’d be by now versus where things are. But “most days” is hard to answer.\n\nSome days I’m just… preoccupied, I guess. Like I’ll sit down to do one thing and then suddenly I’m thinking about three other things, and then I’m irritated at myself for not being able to just handle it like I used to. But down, like sad all day? I don’t know. It kind of comes and goes.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a specific stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, it really started after I got fired… that was about thirteen months ago now, I think. It’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just normal stress, anyone would be spun up after that.\n\nBut then even when I found contract work, it didn’t really settle down. I kept thinking, well, once the next thing starts I’ll calm down, and then once I got something I was still… I don’t know, still carrying it around. And then you start comparing yourself to other people from old teams, LinkedIn updates and all that, which is probably not helpful—sorry. But yeah, the firing was the point where it started, and it hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the reason I came in, honestly. Once it starts, I don’t seem to be able to shut it off.\n\nIt can start with something small, like a client not responding to an email, and then within a few minutes I’m thinking, okay, they’re unhappy, I’m going to lose the contract, then I won’t keep up with the divorce payments, and then I’ll never get back into a real leadership role again. And then I’m checking my phone or replaying what I wrote, which is stupid because staring at it doesn’t make them answer faster.\n\nSo, yes. It’s hard to control. It just kind of runs away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still… I mean, I still like things when I actually do them, I think. It’s more that I don’t always get around to them.\n\nLike I used to watch games or mess around with some side tech projects, and then I’ll open my laptop and somehow end up reading some article about the job market or comparing salaries, which is not fun, obviously. But that’s not the same as not enjoying anything, right?\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of pleasure. It’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. That’s a heavy word, you know?\n\nI have moments where I think, “What if this just doesn’t turn around?” and then I’ll start thinking about work stuff, or people I used to manage who are now doing great, and I get kind of stuck in that comparison loop. But I don’t know if that’s hopeless exactly, or just… feeling worn down.\n\nI guess I haven’t really put that label on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, in the moment it feels totally justified, like I’m just being realistic. But then if I say it out loud… yeah, it’s bigger than the situation.\n\nLike one delayed email from a recruiter, and my mind is already off to, okay, they’re not interested, no one’s interested, my contract work is going to dry up, then how am I going to handle the mortgage, and then I start thinking about people in the Bethesda tech circles and whether they see me as damaged goods now. Which sounds dramatic when I’m saying it here.\n\nIt’s not like the email itself deserves all that. It’s just where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling physically restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… physically, yes. I feel kind of wound up almost all day, honestly.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are just locked up, and I’m sort of bracing for something — like bad news is about to come in, even if nothing’s actually happening. I’ll be on a call and realize I’ve been bouncing my leg the whole time, or I’ll get up and pace around the house for no real reason.\n\nI mean, sometimes I tell myself I’m just “thinking better” while moving around, but… yeah, it’s more like I’m on edge.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. It really started when I got fired, which was about thirteen months ago now.\n\nAnd I thought once I picked up the contract work it would settle down, because, you know, at least I wasn’t just sitting there refreshing job boards and LinkedIn all day — though I still did that more than I should’ve — but it didn’t really let up. The circumstances changed a little, but the worrying just kind of… stayed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued or easily tired because of all this worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired, yes, but I’m not sure I can say it’s specifically because of the worrying.\n\nI mean, I do get drained, but then again I’m 47 and staring at screens half the day, and even normal work stuff can wipe you out. There are days where I’ll sit down after lunch and feel like my battery just dropped to 10%, but I don’t always connect it directly to the worry. It’s all kind of tangled together, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. Most nights I get into bed and my brain just starts running through things, like replaying how I answered an interview question or how I should’ve said something differently.\n\nAnd then I’ll check the clock and it’s like 2 or 3 in the morning, which of course makes it worse because then I’m doing the math on how much sleep I can still get. It reminds me of when I used to be on call for system outages—like even if nothing happens, you’re never really fully off. So even on nights where technically I got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t actually rest.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I try to tell myself I’m still functioning because I’m doing some work, but I’ve missed networking follow-ups that I really shouldn’t have missed. Like I’ll write the email in my head ten different ways and then not send it because I’m thinking of all the ways it could come off wrong.\n\nAnd with client stuff, I’ve delayed deliverables because I get stuck anticipating everything that might go sideways instead of just finishing the thing. It’s stupid because in my line of work you’re supposed to plan for failure points, but this is different — it’s like I can’t stop planning for every possible disaster.\n\nOutside of work too… I’ve pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. I used to go more regularly, and now I just feel tense and irritable, and honestly kind of embarrassed to be around people like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety now is out of proportion to what the job loss itself would explain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is out of proportion. I mean, losing the job is a real thing, obviously, it’s not nothing. But my mind doesn’t stay on just that. It jumps from contract work to the mortgage to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are quietly thinking I’m damaged goods now.\n\nAnd then something small, like a recruiter taking an extra day to email back, feels like… I don’t know, like proof that the whole career is closing down. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
